White can play Bh4 or Be3 or retreat.
If I play g7g6:
White can play Bh4 or Be3.

Let's look at the White Queen on c5. It controls the c-file and diagonals.
White King on d3 is quite advanced.

The legal moves are very limited.
e8d7
h7h6
g7g6
h7h5

h7h6 seems like the most proactive move to kick the Bishop.

Wait, let me double check the board.
Row 1: . . . . Ke8 . . .
Row 2: . . . . . . pg7 ph7
Row 4: . . Qc5 . . . Bg5 .
Row 5: . . Pc4 . . . . .
Row 6: Pa3 . Pc3 Kd3 . . . .
Row 8: Ra1 . . . . Rf1 . .

The bishop on g5 is very annoying. Let's try to push it back.
h7h6 is a standard way to deal with a bishop on g5.

Wait, let's check for immediate threats.
The White Queen is at c5. It can move to c8 (check!) if the king stays at e8.
If I move e8d7, I avoid the immediate Qc8+ threat? No, Qc8 is still a check if the king moves to d7 (it would be on d7, the queen is on c5, that's not a check).
But if King is at e8, Qc8 is check.
If King is at d7, the Queen at c5 doesn't have a direct check on d7.

However, the priority is probably to try and disturb the pieces if possible.
h7h6 forces the bishop to move.

Is there any mating threat?
Queen at c5, Bishop at g5.
If King at e8, moves like Qd5+ or similar are possible.
Wait, White King is at d3. Rooks at a1, f1.

Given the desperate situation, h7h6 is a reasonable try.
